Weak universality for a class of 3d stochastic reaction–diffusion models
M. Furlan CEREMADE Université Paris Dauphine, France Email: furlan@ceremade.dauphine.fr, M. Gubinelli IAM & HCM Universität Bonn, Germany Email: gubinelli@iam.uni-bonn.de

Abstract
We establish the large scale convergence of a class of stochastic weakly nonlinear reaction–diffusion models on a three dimensional periodic domain to the dynamic Φ 3 4 \Phi^{4}_{3} model within the framework of paracontrolled distributions. Our work extends previous results of Hairer and Xu to nonlinearities with a finite amount of smoothness (in particular C 9 C^{9} is enough). We use the Malliavin calculus to perform a partial chaos expansion of the stochastic terms and control their L p L^{p} norms in terms of the graphs of the standard Φ 3 4 \Phi^{4}_{3} stochastic terms.



Keywords:
weak universality, paracontrolled distributions, stochastic
quantisation equation, Malliavin calculus, partial chaos expansion.

1
Introduction

Consider a family of stochastic reaction–diffusion equation in a

weakly nonlinear regime
:

\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptu(t,x)=-\varepsilon^{\alpha}F_{\varepsilon}(u(t,x))+\eta(t,x),\qquad(t,x)\in[0,T/\varepsilon^{2}]\times(\mathbbm{T}/\varepsilon)^{3}

(1.1)

with
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
T>0
, initial condition
\bar{u}_{0,\varepsilon}:(\mathbbm{T}/\varepsilon)^{3}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
,

F_{\varepsilon}\in C^{9}(\mathbbm{R})
with exponential growth at infinity,

\alpha>0
and
\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997pt:=(\partial_{t}-\Delta)
the heat flow operator and

\mathbbm{T}=\mathbbm{R}/(2\pi\mathbbm{Z})
. Here
\eta
denotes a family
of centered Gaussian noises on
[0,T/\varepsilon^{2}]\times(\mathbbm{T}/\varepsilon)^{3}
with stationary covariance

\mathbbm{E}(\eta(t,x)\eta(s,y))=\tilde{C}^{\varepsilon}(t-s,x,y)

such that
\tilde{C}^{\varepsilon}(t-s,x,y)=\Sigma(t-s,x-y)
if

\operatorname{dist}(x,y)\leqslant 1
and
0
otherwise where
\Sigma:\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{+}
is a smooth, positive function
compactly supported in
[0,1]\times B_{\mathbbm{R}^{3}}(0,1)
. We assume
also that there exists a compactly supported function
\psi
such that
\psi\ast\psi=\Sigma
(this is true e.g. when
\eta
is obtained by space-time
convolution of the white noise with
\psi
).

We look for a large scale description of the solution to eq. (
1.1
)
and we introduce the “mesoscopic” scale variable
u_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\varepsilon^{-\beta}u(t/\varepsilon^{2},x/\varepsilon)
where
\beta>0
. Substituting
u_{\varepsilon}
into (
1.1
) we get

\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptu_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=-\varepsilon^{\alpha-2-\beta}F_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\beta}u_{\varepsilon}(t,x))+\varepsilon^{-2-\beta}\eta\left(\frac{t}{\varepsilon^{2}},\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right).

(1.2)

In order for the term
\varepsilon^{-2-\beta}\eta(t/\varepsilon^{2},x/\varepsilon)
to converge to a non–trivial random limit we need that
\beta=1/2
. Indeed the Gaussian field
\eta_{\varepsilon}(t,x):=\varepsilon^{-5/2}\eta(t/\varepsilon^{2},x/\varepsilon)
has
covariance
\varepsilon^{-5}\tilde{C}^{\varepsilon}(t/\varepsilon^{2},x/\varepsilon)
and converges in distribution to the space-time white noise on

\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3}
. For large values of
\alpha
the
non–linearity will be negligible with respect to the additive noise term.
Heuristically, we can attempt an expansion of the reaction term around the
stationary solution
Y_{\varepsilon}
to the linear equation

\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptY_{\varepsilon}=-Y_{\varepsilon}+\eta_{\varepsilon},

(1.3)

i.e.
Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\check{P}(t-s,x-y)\eta_{\varepsilon}(s,y)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}y
with
\check{P}(t,x)=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{|x|^{2}}{4t}}e^{-t}\mathbbm{1}_{t\geqslant 0}
.

Let us denote with
C_{\varepsilon}
the covariance of
Y_{\varepsilon}
. We
approximate the reaction term as

\varepsilon^{\alpha-5/2}F_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}u_{\varepsilon}(t,x))\simeq\varepsilon^{\alpha-5/2}F_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x)).

The Gaussian r.v.
\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x)
has variance

\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=\varepsilon\mathbbm{E}[(Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x))^{2}]=\varepsilon\mathbbm{E}[(Y_{\varepsilon}(0,0))^{2}]=\varepsilon C_{\varepsilon}(0,0)
independent of
(t,x)
. Although

\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
depends on
\varepsilon
, it can be bounded from
above and below by two positive constants uniformly on
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
. We can expand the random variable
F_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x))
according to the chaos decomposition relative to

\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x)
and obtain

F_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x))=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}f_{n,\varepsilon}H_{n}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x),\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}),

(1.4)

where
H_{n}(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})
are standard Hermite polynomials with
variance
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
and highest-order term normalized to
1
.
Note also that the coefficients
(f_{n,\varepsilon})_{n\geqslant 0}
do not
depend on
(t,x)
by stationarity of the law of
\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x)
since they are given by the formula

f_{n,\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{n!\sigma^{2n}_{\varepsilon}}\mathbbm{E}[F_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x))H_{n}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x),\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})]=n!\mathbbm{E}[F_{\varepsilon}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}G)H_{n}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}G,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})]

where
G
is a standard Gaussian variable of unit variance.

Let
X
be the stationary solution to the equation

\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptX=-X+\xi,

with
\xi
the space–time white noise on
\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3}

and denote by
\llbracket X^{N}\rrbracket
the generalized random fields given
by the
N
-th Wick power of
X
, which are well defined as random elements of

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3})
as long as
N\leqslant 4
.
Denote with
C_{X}
the covariance of
X
. The Gaussian analysis which we set up
in this paper shows in particular that if
\varepsilon^{(n-N)/2}f_{n,\varepsilon}\rightarrow g_{n}
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
for every
0\leqslant n\leqslant N
,
N\leqslant 4
, and

(F_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subseteq C^{N+1}(\mathbbm{R})
with
exponential growth, then the family of random fields

\mathbbm{F}^{N}_{\varepsilon}:(t,x)\mapsto\varepsilon^{-N/2}F_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x)),\qquad(t,x)\in\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3},

converges in law in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3})
as

\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
to
\sum_{n=0}^{N}g_{n}\llbracket X^{n}\rrbracket
.

Consider the smallest
n
such that
f_{n,\varepsilon}
converges to a finite
limit as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Since
H_{n}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon},\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})=\varepsilon^{n/2}\llbracket Y_{\varepsilon}^{n}\rrbracket
, the
n
-th term in the
expansion (
1.4
) is
f_{n,\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{\alpha+(n-5)/2}\llbracket Y_{\varepsilon}^{n}\rrbracket
. Therefore, the equation
yields a non-trivial limit only if
\alpha=(5-n)/2
. We are interested
mainly in the case
n=3\Rightarrow\alpha=1
and
n=1\Rightarrow\alpha=2
. The case
\alpha=2
gives rise to a Gaussian limit and its
analysis its not very difficult. In the following we will concentrate in the
analysis of the
\alpha=1
case where the limiting behaviour of the model is
the most interesting and given by the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
family of singular SPDEs. In
this case we obtain the family of models

\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptu_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=-\varepsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}F_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{\varepsilon}(t,x))+\eta_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\quad

(1.5)

with initial condition
u_{0,\varepsilon}(\cdot):=\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{u}_{0,\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot)
where

\bar{u}_{0,\varepsilon}
is the initial condition of the microscopic
model (
1.1
).

In order to state our main result, Theorem
1.1
below, let us
introduce some notations and specify our assumptions. Let

\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}
be the centering (up to the third Wiener chaos
relative to
\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x)
) of the function

F_{\varepsilon}
, i.e.

\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(x):=F_{\varepsilon}(x)-f_{0,\varepsilon}-f_{1,\varepsilon}x-f_{2,\varepsilon}H_{2}(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})=\sum_{n\geqslant 3}f_{n,\varepsilon}H_{n}(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}).

(1.6)

The decomposition of
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}
is obviously the same as
in (
1.4
) except for the fact that we have discarded the orders
0,1,2. Let
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(m)}
be the
m
-th derivative of the
function
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}
for
0\leqslant m\leqslant 9
and define
the following
\varepsilon
–dependent constants:

\begin{array}[]{lll}d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}&:=&\frac{\varepsilon^{-2}}{9}\int_{s,x}P_{s}(x)\mathbbm{E}[\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(s,x))\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(0,0))],\\
\bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}&:=&2\varepsilon^{-1/2}f_{3,\varepsilon}f_{2,\varepsilon}\int_{s,x}P_{s}(x)(C_{\varepsilon}(s,x))^{2},\\
d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}&:=&\frac{\varepsilon^{-2}}{6}\int_{s,x}P_{s}(x)\mathbbm{E}[\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(0)}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(s,x))\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(0,0))],\\
d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}&:=&\frac{\varepsilon^{-5/2}}{3}\int_{s,x}P_{s}(x)\mathbbm{E}[\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(0)}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(s,x))\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}(0,0))],\end{array}

(1.7)

where
P_{s}(x)
is the heat kernel and
\int_{s,x}
denotes integration on

\mathbbm{R}^{+}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3}
.

Assumption 1
.

All along the paper we enforce the following assumptions:

a)

(u_{0,\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
converges in law to a limit

u_{0}
in
\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-1/2-\kappa}
and is independent of
\eta
;

b)

(\bar{u}_{0,\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded in

L^{\infty}
in probability, i.e.
\exists C>0
such that
\forall\varepsilon\in(0,1]

\|\bar{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}((\mathbbm{T}/\varepsilon)^{3})}\leqslant C
;

c)

(F_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subseteq C^{9}(\mathbbm{R})
and
there exists constants
c,C>0
such that

\sup_{\varepsilon,x}\sum_{k=0}^{9}|\partial_{x}^{k}F_{\varepsilon}(x)|\leqslant Ce^{c|x|},

(1.8)

d)

the family of vectors
\lambda_{\varepsilon}=(\lambda_{0,\varepsilon},\lambda_{1,\varepsilon},\lambda_{2,\varepsilon},\lambda_{3,\varepsilon})\in\mathbbm{R}^{4}
given by

\begin{array}[]{lllllll}\lambda_{3,\varepsilon}&:=&f_{3,\varepsilon}&&\lambda_{1,\varepsilon}&:=&\varepsilon^{-1}f_{1,\varepsilon}-9d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{830133}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}-6d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{830133}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}\\
\lambda_{2,\varepsilon}&:=&\varepsilon^{-1/2}f_{2,\varepsilon}&&\lambda_{0,\varepsilon}&:=&\varepsilon^{-3/2}f_{0,\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{-1/2}f_{2,\varepsilon}d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{830133}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}-3d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{830133}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}-3\bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{830133}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}\end{array}

(1.9)

has a finite limit
\lambda=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in\mathbbm{R}^{4}
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.

Theorem 1.1
(Convergence of the solution).

Under Assumption
1
the family of random
fields
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
given by the solution to
eq. (
1.5
) converges in law and locally in time to a limiting random
field
u(\lambda)
in the space
C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha}(\mathbbm{T}^{3})
for
every
1/2\char 60\relax\alpha\char 60\relax 2/3
. The law of
u(\lambda)
depends only on the
value of
\lambda
and not on the other details of the nonlinearity or on
the covariance of the noise term. We call this limit the dynamic
\Phi^{4}_{3}

model with parameter vector
\lambda\in\mathbbm{R}^{4}
.

Here
C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha}(\mathbbm{T}^{3})
denotes the space of continuous
functions from
[0,T]
to the Besov space
\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha}(\mathbbm{T}^{3})=B^{-\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbbm{T}^{3})
(see Appendix
A.1

for the notation on Besov spaces and paraproducts).
Theorem
1.1
is actually just a corollary of the more precise
result Theorem
2.2
, in which we identify the paracontrolled
equation satisfied by the limit random field
u(\lambda)
.

Remark 1.2
.

We are interested only in local-in-time convergence of
u_{\varepsilon}
, as
a way to show the potential of our method for controlling stochastic terms
with infinite chaos decomposition (developed in Section
3
).
Nevertheless, we expect it to be possible to obtain global-in-time
convergence of the solution with more stringent assumptions on

F_{\varepsilon}
, although we do not treat this problem here.

Remark 1.3
.

As a special case we can take

\begin{array}[]{lll}F_{\varepsilon}(x)&=&\lambda_{3}H_{3}(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})+\varepsilon^{1/2}\lambda_{2}H_{2}(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})+\varepsilon(\lambda_{1}+\gamma_{1,\varepsilon})H_{1}(x,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})+\varepsilon^{3/2}(\lambda_{0}+\gamma_{0,\varepsilon})\end{array}

so that

f_{3,\varepsilon}=\lambda_{3},\quad\varepsilon^{-1/2}f_{2,\varepsilon}=\lambda_{2},\quad\varepsilon^{-1}f_{1,\varepsilon}=\lambda_{1}+\gamma_{1,\varepsilon},\quad\varepsilon^{-3/2}f_{0,\varepsilon}=\lambda_{0}+\gamma_{0,\varepsilon},

and

d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}=(\lambda_{3})^{2}L_{\varepsilon},\quad\bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}=\lambda_{3}\lambda_{2}L_{\varepsilon},\quad d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}=d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}=0,

where
L_{\varepsilon}:=2\int_{s,x}P_{s}(x)(C_{Y,\varepsilon}(s,x))^{2}.
Choosing

\gamma_{1,\varepsilon}:=9d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}=9(\lambda_{3})^{2}L_{\varepsilon},\qquad\gamma_{0,\varepsilon}:=3\bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}=3\lambda_{3}\lambda_{2}L_{\varepsilon},

we obtain
\lambda_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
. This shows that all the possible limits
\lambda\in\mathbbm{R}^{4}
are attainable. In this case (
1.5
) takes the form

\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptu_{\varepsilon}=-\lambda_{3}u_{\varepsilon}^{3}-\lambda_{2}u_{\varepsilon}^{2}-[\lambda_{1}-3\lambda_{3}\varepsilon^{-1}\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon}+9(\lambda_{3})^{2}L_{\varepsilon}]u_{\varepsilon}-\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{2}\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon}-3\lambda_{3}\lambda_{2}L_{\varepsilon}+\eta_{\varepsilon}.

(1.10)

The name dynamic
\Phi^{4}_{3}
equation (or stochastic quantisation equation)
derives from the fact that the simplest class of models which approximate
the limiting random field
u(\lambda)
is precisely obtained by choosing a
cubic polynomial like in (
1.10
) as non-linear term (which is the
gradient of a fourth order polinomial playing the role of local potential).

In two dimensions, this model has been subject of various studies since more
than thirty
years
[
12
,
1
,
5
]
.
For the three dimensional case, the kind of convergence results described
above are originally due to
Hairer
[
8
,
9
]
and constitute one
of the first groundbreaking applications of his theory of regularity
structures. Similar results were later obtained by Catellier and
Chouk
[
4
]
using the paracontrolled approach
of Gubinelli, Imkeller and Perkowski
[
6
]
.
Kupiainen
[
13
]
described a third approach
using renormalization group ideas.

Weak universality
is the observation that the same limiting object
describes the large scale behaviour of the solutions of more general
equations, in particular that of the many parameters present in a general
model, only a finite number of their combinations survive in the limit to
describe the limiting object. The adjective “weak” is related to the fact
that in order to control the large scale limit the non-linearity has to be
very small in the microscopic scale. This sets up a perturbative regime which
is well suited to the analysis via regularity structures or paracontrolled
distributions.

The first result of weak universality for a singular stochastic PDE has been
given by Hairer and Quastel
[
10
]
in the context the
Kardar–Parisi–Zhang equation. Using the machinery developed there Hairer and
Wu
[
11
]
proved a weak universality result for three
dimensional reaction–diffusion equations in the case of Gaussian noise and a
polynomial non–linearity, within the context of regularity structures. Weak
universality for reaction–diffusion equations driven by non Gaussian noise is
analysed in Shen and Wu
[
22
]
. Recently, important results
concerning the stochastic quantisation equation we obtained by Mourrat and
Weber. In particular the convergence to the dynamic
\Phi^{4}_{2}
model for a
class of Markovian dynamics of discrete spin
systems
[
15
]
and also the global wellposedness of

\Phi^{4}_{2}
in space and time
[
16
]
and in time for

\Phi^{4}_{3}

[
17
]
. The recent
preprint
[
21
]
analyzes an hyperbolic
version of the stochastic quantisation equation in two dimensions, including
the associated universality in the small noise regime.

The present work is the first which considers in detail the weak universality
problem in the context of paracontrolled distributions, showing that on the
analytic side the apriori estimates can be obtained via standard arguments and
that the major difficulty is related to showing the convergence of a finite
number of random fields to universal limiting objects. The main novelty of our
work is our use of the Malliavin
calculus
[
20
,
19
]
to perform the analysis of
these stochastic terms without requiring polynomial non–linearity as in the
previous works cited above. In particular we were inspired by the computations
in
[
25
]
and in general by the use of the Malliavin
calculus to establish normal approximations
[
19
]
. The
main technical results of our paper, Theorem
3.1
below, is not
particularly linked to paracontrolled distributions. A similar analysis is
conceivable for the stochastic models in regularity structures. Moreover the
same tools can also allow to prove similar non-polynomial weak universality
statements for the KPZ along the lines of the present analysis. This is the
subject of ongoing work.

Acknowledgments
.

The authors would like to thank the anonymous referee for the detailed and
constructive critique which contributed to improve the overall exposition of
the results. Support via SFB CRC 1060 is also gratefully acknowledged.

2
Analysis of the mesoscopic model

The goal of this section is to obtain a paracontrolled structure for
equation (
1.5
) analogous to that introduced
in
[
4
]
for the cubic polynomial case and
use it to set up the limiting procedure. Convergence of the stochastic terms
and some apriori estimates will be the subject of the following sections.
Definitions and a reminder of the basic results of paradifferential calculus
needed here can be found in Appendix
A
.

2.1
Paracontrolled structure

Let us start our analysis by centering the reaction term
F_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}u_{\varepsilon})
in (
1.5
) using
decomposition (
1.6
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptu_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{\varepsilon}(t,x))+\eta_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{-3/2}f_{0,\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{-1}f_{1,\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{-3/2}f_{2,\varepsilon}H_{2}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{\varepsilon},\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right).

We write
u_{\varepsilon}=Y_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon}
with

Y_{\varepsilon}
as in (
1.3
), and perform a Taylor expansion of

\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{1/2}v_{\varepsilon})
around
\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}
up to the
third order to get

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptu_{\varepsilon}&=&\eta_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon^{-1}\tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})v_{\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{F}^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})v_{\varepsilon}^{2}-\frac{1}{6}\tilde{F}^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})v_{\varepsilon}^{3}\\
&&-\varepsilon^{-3/2}f_{0,\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{-1}f_{1,\varepsilon}(Y_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon^{-1/2}f_{2,\varepsilon}(\llbracket Y_{\varepsilon}^{2}\rrbracket+2v_{\varepsilon}Y_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon}^{2})-R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}).\end{array}

(2.1)

where
R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})
is the remainder of the Taylor
series and we used the fact that
H_{2}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon},\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})=\varepsilon\llbracket Y_{\varepsilon}^{2}\rrbracket
. Notice that we stopped the Taylor expansion at the first term for
which
\varepsilon
does not appear anymore with a negative exponent (that is

\tilde{F}^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})
).
One can then expect the remainder
R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})
to
converge to zero in some sense. On the other hand, all the other terms except

\tilde{F}^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})

and
R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})
appear to diverge in the limit

\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, but in analogy with well-known renormalization
methods for random fields, we try to find a combination of them that can be
made to converge in some function space. Define the following random fields:

\begin{array}[]{lllllll}\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptY_{\varepsilon}&:=&-Y_{\varepsilon}+\eta_{\varepsilon}&&&&\\
\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}&:=&\varepsilon^{-1/2}f_{2,\varepsilon}\llbracket Y_{\varepsilon}^{2}\rrbracket&&\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997pt\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}&:=&\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}},\\
Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/3_tree.eps}}}&:=&\varepsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})&&\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptY_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}&:=&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/3_tree.eps}}},\\
Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}&:=&\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon^{-1}\tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})&&\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptY_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}&:=&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}\\
Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}&:=&\frac{1}{6}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{F}^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})&&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing}&:=&\frac{1}{6}\tilde{F}^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})\\
\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}&:=&\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}-\bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}&&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}&:=&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}-d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}},\\
Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}&:=&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}-d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}},&&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}&:=&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}-d_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}Y_{\varepsilon}-d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}},\end{array}

(2.2)

with
Y_{\varepsilon}
stationary solution, while
Y_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}},\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}
have
0
initial condition in
t=0
. The
last four trees
\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}
,

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}
,
Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}
,

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}
are obtained from the others via the resonant
Bony’s paraproduct
\circ
recalled in Appendix
A
, and

\bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}
,
d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}
,

d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}
,
d_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}
,

d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}
are just
\varepsilon
-dependent constants
whose exact value will matter only in Section
3
. Indeed, in
the scope of this section we only need the following relation to be verified:

\begin{array}[]{lll}d_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}&=&2d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}+3d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}.\end{array}

(2.3)

The notation
\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}
denotes that this tree has finite
chaos expansion and can be treated with the well-known techniques
of
[
4
]

or
[
18
]
(we put a bar on

\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}
just because is the only tree obtained
from
\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}
)

With the definitions (
2.2
), equation (
2.1
) takes
the form

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptv_{\varepsilon}&=&Y_{\varepsilon}-\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/3_tree.eps}}}-3Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}v_{\varepsilon}-3Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}v_{\varepsilon}^{2}-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing}v_{\varepsilon}^{3}\\
&&-\varepsilon^{-3/2}f_{0,\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{-1}f_{1,\varepsilon}(Y_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon^{-1/2}f_{2,\varepsilon}(2Y_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon}^{2})-R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}).\end{array}

(2.4)

At this point it is worth noting that the trivial case

\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(x)=H_{3}(x,\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon})
yields

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/3_tree.eps}}}=\llbracket Y_{\varepsilon}^{3}\rrbracket
,

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}=\llbracket Y^{2}_{\varepsilon}\rrbracket
,

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}=Y_{\varepsilon}
,
Y^{\varnothing}=1
. By comparing
these random fields to the ones defined
in
[
4
]
we can
guess
that

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}},Y^{\varnothing}
can be controlled respectively in
\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{1/2-\kappa},\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-1-\kappa},\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-1/2-\kappa},\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\kappa}

\forall\kappa>0
for
any
F_{\varepsilon}
satisfying Assumption
1
, and carry on the
paracontrolled analysis of (
2.4
) as if it were the case. Clearly, the
paracontrolled structure is robust and does not depend on how the terms

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}},Y^{\varnothing}
are defined as long as they have the desired regularity.

From these observations, we do not expect to be able to control the products

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}v_{\varepsilon}
,
Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}v_{\varepsilon}^{2}
and
Y_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}
in eq. (
2.4
)
uniformly in
\varepsilon>0
. In order to proceed with the analysis we make
the Ansatz:

\begin{array}[]{lll}u_{\varepsilon}&=&Y_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon},\\
v_{\varepsilon}&=&-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}-\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}-3v_{\varepsilon}\prec\!\!\!\prec Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}+v^{\natural}_{\varepsilon}\end{array}

(2.5)

and proceed to decompose the ill-defined products using the paracontrolled
techniques recalled in Appendix
A
. We start by writing

v_{\varepsilon}Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}=v_{\varepsilon}\prec Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}+v_{\varepsilon}\succ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}+v_{\varepsilon}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}
. The resonant term, together
with Ansatz (
2.5
), yields:

\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}-\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}-3v_{\varepsilon}(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}})

\displaystyle-3\overline{\operatorname{com}}_{1}(v_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}})+v^{\natural}_{\varepsilon}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}},

with the definition and bounds of
\overline{\operatorname{com}}_{1}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
given in Lemma
A.7
. Then we define

\begin{array}[]{lll}Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}\hat{\diamond}v_{\varepsilon}&:=&v_{\varepsilon}Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}-v_{\varepsilon}\prec Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}+(3v_{\varepsilon}d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}+d_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}Y_{\varepsilon}+d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}+\bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}})\\
&=&v_{\varepsilon}\succ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}-\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}-3v_{\varepsilon}Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}+v^{\natural}_{\varepsilon}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}-3\overline{\operatorname{com}}_{1}(v_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}).\end{array}

Moreover we have for
v_{\varepsilon}Y_{\varepsilon}
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}v_{\varepsilon}Y_{\varepsilon}&=&\varphi_{\varepsilon}Y_{\varepsilon}-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}\prec Y_{\varepsilon}-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}\succ Y_{\varepsilon}-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}\circ Y_{\varepsilon},\end{array}

where we introduced the shorthand
\varphi_{\varepsilon}:=v_{\varepsilon}+Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}
. So we let

v_{\varepsilon}\diamond Y_{\varepsilon}:=v_{\varepsilon}Y_{\varepsilon}+d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}=\varphi_{\varepsilon}Y_{\varepsilon}-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}\prec Y_{\varepsilon}-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}\succ Y_{\varepsilon}-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}},

Finally to analyse the product
Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}v_{\varepsilon}^{2}
we
write

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}v_{\varepsilon}^{2}=Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}})^{2}-2Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}+Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{2},

and consider the products involving only
Y^{\tau}
factors: first

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}=Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\succ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}+Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\prec Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}+Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}+d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}=:Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\diamond Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}+d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}},

and then we define the term
Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\diamond(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}})^{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\diamond(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}})^{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}})^{2}-2d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\prec(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}})^{2}+Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\succ(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}})^{2}+Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\circ(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}\circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}})+2\operatorname{com}_{1}(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}})+2Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}},

so that

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\diamond v_{\varepsilon}^{2}:=Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}v_{\varepsilon}^{2}+2d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}v_{\varepsilon}=Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\diamond(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}})^{2}-2(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\diamond Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}})\varphi_{\varepsilon}+Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{2}.

We note also that

\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptv_{\varepsilon}=-\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptY_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}-\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997pt\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}+\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptv^{\natural}_{\varepsilon}-3v_{\varepsilon}\prec\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptY_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}-3\operatorname{com}_{3}(v_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}})-3\operatorname{com}_{2}(v_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}),

with
\operatorname{com}_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\operatorname{com}_{3}(\cdot,\cdot)

specified in Lemma
A.7
. Substituting
these renormalized products into (
2.4
) and recalling the
definition (
1.9
) for
\lambda_{\varepsilon}=(\lambda_{0,\varepsilon},\lambda_{1,\varepsilon},\lambda_{2,\varepsilon},\lambda_{3,\varepsilon})
, we obtain the following equation for

v^{\natural}_{\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptv_{\varepsilon}^{\natural}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\operatorname{com}_{3}(v_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}})+3\operatorname{com}_{2}(v_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}})

\displaystyle-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing}v_{\varepsilon}^{3}-3Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\diamond v_{\varepsilon}^{2}-3Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}\hat{\diamond}v_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+Y_{\varepsilon}-\lambda_{2,\varepsilon}(2v_{\varepsilon}\diamond Y_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon}^{2})

\displaystyle-\lambda_{1,\varepsilon}(Y_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon})+[9d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}+6d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}-3d_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}]v_{\varepsilon}-\lambda_{0,\varepsilon}-R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}),

where we can use the constraint (
2.3
) to remove the term
proportional to
v_{\varepsilon}
. Summarizing, we obtain the following
equation, together with Ansatz (
2.5
):

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}v_{\varepsilon}&=&-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}-\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}-3v_{\varepsilon}\prec\!\!\!\prec Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}+v^{\natural}\\
\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptv_{\varepsilon}^{\natural}&=&U(\lambda_{\varepsilon},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon};v_{\varepsilon},v_{\varepsilon}^{\natural})-R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\end{array}\right.

(2.6)

with initial condition
v_{\varepsilon,0}=u_{0,\varepsilon}-Y_{\varepsilon}(0)
and
U
given by

\begin{array}[]{lll}U(\lambda_{\varepsilon},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon};v_{\varepsilon},v_{\varepsilon}^{\natural})&:=&3\operatorname{com}_{3}(v_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}})+3\operatorname{com}_{2}(v_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}})-Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing}v_{\varepsilon}^{3}\\
&&-3Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}\diamond v_{\varepsilon}^{2}-3Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}\hat{\diamond}v_{\varepsilon}+Y_{\varepsilon}-\lambda_{2,\varepsilon}(2v_{\varepsilon}\diamond Y_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon}^{2})\\
&&-\lambda_{1,\varepsilon}(Y_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon})-\lambda_{0,\varepsilon}-R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}).\end{array}

(2.7)

The enhanced noise vector
\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}},\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}},\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}})

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{T}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\kappa}\times C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\frac{1}{2}-\kappa}\times\left(C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-1-\kappa}\right)^{2}\times\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997pt_{T}^{1/2-\kappa}\times\left(C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\kappa}\right)^{3}\times C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\frac{1}{2}-\kappa}

(2.8)

for every
\kappa>0
,
T>0
. We use the notation
\|\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{T}}=\sum_{\tau}\|\mathbbm{Y}^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{X}^{\tau}}
for the associated
norm where
Y_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}
is a generic tree in

\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}
. The homogeneities
|\tau|\in\mathbbm{R}
are
given by

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr Y_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}&=&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing}&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}&\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}&\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}&Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}\\
\hline\cr|\tau|&=&0&-1/2&-1&-1&1/2&0&0&0&-1/2\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Note that for every
\varepsilon>0
eq. (
2.6
) is
equivalent to eq. (
1.5
).

Remark 2.1
.

The paracontrolled structure we developed in this
section is the same as in the work of Catellier and
Chouk
[
4
]
, plus an additive source term
(which is
R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})
in
equation (
2.6
)). Therefore, there exists
T=T\left(\|\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{T}},\|u_{\varepsilon,0}\|_{\mathscr{C}\hskip 0.81949pt^{-1/2-\kappa}},|\lambda_{\varepsilon}|\right)
such that we
can define for
\alpha\in(1/2,2/3)
,
p\in[4,\infty)
,
\gamma>\frac{1}{4}+\frac{3}{2}\kappa
a solution map

\begin{array}[]{llll}\Gamma:&\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-1/2-\kappa}\times\mathcal{X}^{\tau}\times\mathbbm{R}^{4}\times\mathcal{M}^{\gamma,p}_{T}L^{p}(\mathbbm{T}^{3})&\rightarrow&C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha}(\mathbbm{T}^{3})\\
&(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon},\lambda_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{R})&\mapsto&u_{\varepsilon}\end{array}

so that
u_{\varepsilon}=\Gamma(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon},\lambda_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{R})
with

u_{\varepsilon}=Y_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon}
and
v_{\varepsilon}

that solves (
2.6
) with the remainder
R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})
replaced by
\mathcal{R}
. The space

\mathcal{M}^{\gamma}_{T}L^{p}(\mathbbm{T}^{3})
is specified in
Appendix
A.1
. Indeed, we can use Lemma
A.1
and
Lemma
A.3
to control
I\mathcal{R}
as

\|I\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.63885pt^{1/2,1+2\kappa}_{T}}\lesssim T^{\delta}\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathcal{M}^{\gamma,p}_{T}L^{p}}

for
\delta>0
small enough, and thanks to this bound it is easy to see
that the the fixed point procedure of Section 3
of
[
4
]
still holds with a

fixed
additive source term
\mathcal{R}
. In the same way, the
continuity of the solution map
\Gamma
follows easily as in Theorem 1.2
of
[
4
]
.

2.2
Identification of the limit

In order to identify interesting limits for equation (
1.5
), we
introduce
\forall\lambda=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in\mathbbm{R}^{4}
the enhanced noise
\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda)
which is constructed
from universal noises
X^{\tau}
as

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda)&:=&(Y^{\varnothing}(\lambda),Y^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}(\lambda),Y^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(\lambda),\bar{Y}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(\lambda),Y^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}(\lambda),Y^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}(\lambda),Y^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}(\lambda),Y^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}(\lambda),Y^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}(\lambda))\\
&:=&(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{3}X,\lambda_{3}X^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}},\lambda_{2}X^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}},\lambda_{3}X^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}},(\lambda_{3})^{2}X^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}},(\lambda_{3})^{2}X^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}},\lambda_{3}\lambda_{2}X^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}},(\lambda_{3})^{2}X^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}})\end{array}

(2.9)

where
X
is the stationary solution to to the linear equation
\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptX=-X+\xi
and
\xi
is the time-space white noise on
\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3}
. We will sometimes use the shorter notation
\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda)=(Y^{\tau}(\lambda))_{\tau}
for (
2.9
).

We define the universal fields
X^{\tau}
through their Littlewood-Paley
decomposition
\forall(t,\bar{x})\in\mathbbm{R}^{+}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3}

as:

\begin{array}[]{lll}X^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/3_tree.eps}}}&:=&\llbracket X^{3}\rrbracket,\qquad\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptX^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}=X^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/3_tree.eps}}}\quad\text{with $X^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}(t=0)=0$},\\
X^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}&:=&\llbracket X^{2}\rrbracket,\\
\Delta_{q}X^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}}(t,\bar{x})&:=&\Delta_{q}(1-J_{0})(X^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}\circ X^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}})(t,\bar{x})=\int_{\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}}(1-J_{0})(\llbracket X^{2}(\zeta_{1})\rrbracket\llbracket X^{2}(\zeta_{2})\rrbracket)\mu_{q,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}},\\
\Delta_{q}X^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}}(t,\bar{x})&:=&\Delta_{q}(X^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}\circ X)(t,\bar{x})=\int_{\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}}\llbracket X^{3}(\zeta_{1})\rrbracket X(\zeta_{2})\mu_{q,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}},\\
\Delta_{q}X^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}(t,\bar{x})&:=&\int_{\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}}(1-J_{1})(\llbracket X^{3}(\zeta_{1})\rrbracket\llbracket X^{2}(\zeta_{2})\rrbracket)\mu_{q,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}}\\
&&+6\int_{s,x}[\Delta_{q}X(t+s,\bar{x}-x)-\Delta_{q}X(t,\bar{x})]P_{s}(x)[C(s,x)]^{2},\end{array}

(2.10)

where as before
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
stands for the Wick product,

\zeta_{i}=(x_{i},s_{i})\in\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3}
,
C(\cdot,\cdot)
is the covariance of
X
and
\mu_{q,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}}
is the
measure

\mu_{q,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}}:=\delta(t-s_{2})\mathrm{d}\zeta_{1}\mathrm{d}\zeta_{2}\int_{x,y}K_{q,\bar{x}}(x)\sum_{i\sim j}K_{i,x}(y)K_{j,x}(x_{2})P_{t-s_{1}}(y-x_{1})

with the usual heat kernel
P_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{|x|^{2}}{4t}}\mathbbm{1}_{t\geqslant 0}
. We commit an abuse of
notation by writing
X(\zeta)
since
X
is actually a distribution in space:
the integrals in (
2.10
) should obviously be intended as
functionals.

Standard computations (see e.g.
[
4
]

or
[
18
]
) show that,
\forall\lambda\in\mathbbm{R}^{4}
and for any
T>0
,
0\char 60\relax\kappa\char 60\relax\kappa^{\prime}

\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda)\in C^{\kappa}_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\frac{1}{2}-2\kappa^{\prime}}\times\left(C^{\kappa}_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-1-2\kappa^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\times C^{\kappa}_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{\frac{1}{2}-2\kappa^{\prime}}\times\left(C^{\kappa}_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{0-2\kappa^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\times C^{\kappa}_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\frac{1}{2}-2\kappa^{\prime}}_{T},

almost surely.

Using the paracontrolled structure we developed in
Section
2.1
we can identify the limiting solution
u(\lambda)
introduced in Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 2.2
.

The family of random fields
u_{\varepsilon}
given by
the solutions of eq. (
1.5
) converges in law and locally in time to
a limiting random field
u(\lambda)
in the space
C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha}(\mathbbm{T}^{3})
for every
1/2\char 60\relax\alpha\char 60\relax 2/3
. The limiting random
field
u(\lambda)
solves the paracontrolled equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}u(\lambda)&=&X+v(\lambda)\\
v(\lambda)&=&-\lambda_{3}X^{\resizebox{458753}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}-\lambda_{2}X^{\resizebox{458753}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}-3\lambda_{3}v(\lambda)\prec\!\!\!\prec X^{\resizebox{458753}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}+v^{\natural}(\lambda)\\
\mathscr{L}\hskip 1.99997ptv^{\natural}(\lambda)&=&U(\lambda,\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda);v(\lambda),v^{\natural}(\lambda))\\
v^{\natural}(\lambda)(t=0)&=&v_{0}+\lambda_{3}X^{\resizebox{458753}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}(t=0)+\lambda_{2}X^{\resizebox{458753}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}(t=0)+3\lambda_{3}v_{\varepsilon,0}\prec X^{\resizebox{458753}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/21_tree_mod.eps}}}(t=0)\end{array}\right.

(2.11)

with
U
defined in (
2.7
) and
v_{0}=u_{0}-X(t=0)
.

Proof.

Fix
T>0
. Let
u_{\varepsilon}=Y_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon}
be
the solution of eq. (
1.5
) for fixed
\varepsilon>0
, which is
seen to be unique in the (
\varepsilon
-dependent) time interval
[0,T_{\varepsilon}]
by a usual fixed-point argument on the original equation
(without resorting to the paracontrolled decomposition). Let

u_{\varepsilon}=\Gamma(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon},\lambda_{\varepsilon},R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}))
on
[0,T_{\varepsilon}]
with
\Gamma
defined as in Remark
2.1
and

R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})
seen as an exogenous source term. We
know from the a-priori estimations of Section
4.2
that there
exists a time
T_{\star}=T_{\star}\left(\|\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{T}},\|u_{\varepsilon,0}\|_{\mathscr{C}\hskip 0.81949pt^{-1/2-\kappa}},|\lambda_{\varepsilon}|\right)
and a family of events

(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
such that
\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon})\rightarrow 1
for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0

and we can control
\|v_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{M}^{1/4+3\kappa/2}_{T_{\star}}L^{\infty}}
. Thus, we can control the
L^{\infty}
norm of

v_{\varepsilon}(t)
in
[T_{\varepsilon}/2,T_{\varepsilon}]
and extend
the solution
v_{\varepsilon}
on
[0,T_{\star}]
for every
\varepsilon
.
Denote by
u_{\varepsilon}^{\star}=\Gamma_{\star}(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon},\lambda_{\varepsilon},R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}))
the process
u_{\varepsilon}
stopped at time

T_{\star}
and
\Gamma_{\star}
the corresponding stopped solution map.

Note that
u(\lambda)
solves the same equation as

u_{\varepsilon}^{\star}
with
\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}
replaced by

\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda)
,
u_{\varepsilon,0}
replaced by
u_{0}
,

\lambda_{\varepsilon}
replaced by
\lambda
and
R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})=0
. So
u(\lambda)=u^{\star}=\Gamma_{\star}(u_{0},\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda),\lambda,0)
up to time
T_{\star}
. Let us introduce
the random field
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\star}=\Gamma_{\star}(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon},\lambda_{\varepsilon},0)

with
\bar{v}^{\star}_{\varepsilon}=\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\star}-Y_{\varepsilon}
that solves the paracontrolled
equation (
2.6
) but with remainder
R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})=0
.

Consider the
n
-tuple of random variables
(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon}^{\star},\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\star})
and let
\mu_{\varepsilon}
be its law on

\mathcal{Z}=\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha}\times\mathcal{X}_{T}\times\left(C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha}\right)^{2}
conditionally on
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
. Observe
that
\Gamma_{\star}
is continuous as discussed in
Remark
2.1
, and this gives that
\forall\delta>0
,

\mu_{\varepsilon}\left(\|u_{\varepsilon}^{\star}-\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\star}\|_{C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 0.81949pt^{-\alpha}}>\delta\right)\rightarrow 0
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Indeed
R_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\rightarrow 0
in probability in the space

\mathcal{M}^{\gamma,p}_{T_{\star}}L^{p}(\mathbbm{T}^{3})
by
Lemma
4.5
. This shows that
\mu_{\varepsilon}
concentrates
on
\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha}\times\mathcal{X}_{T}\times\left\{(z,z)\in\left(C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha}\right)^{2}\right\}
. Let
\mu
any accumulation point of

(\mu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
. Then
\mu\left(\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha}\times\mathcal{X}_{T}\times\left\{(z,z)\in\left(C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha}\right)^{2}\right\}\right)=1
. The apriori estimations of Section
4.2

yield the tightness of
\mu_{\varepsilon}
and from the concentration of

\mu
on the diagonal we know that there exists a subsequence such that for
any test function
\varphi
,

\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\varphi(x,y,z,t)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}(x,y,z,t)\rightarrow\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\varphi(x,y,z,t)\mathrm{d}\mu(x,y,z,t)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\varphi(x,y,t,t)\mathrm{d}\mu(x,y,z,t).

(2.12)

Moreover, still along subsequences we have that for any bounded continuous
function
\varphi

\mathbbm{E}(\varphi(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon},\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\star}))=\mathbbm{E}(\varphi(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon},\Gamma_{\star}(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon},\lambda_{\varepsilon},0)))\rightarrow\mathbbm{E}(\varphi(u_{0},\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda),\Gamma_{\star}(u_{0},\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda),\lambda,0)))

since by Theorem
3.1
the vector
\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}

converges in law to
\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda)
,
u_{\varepsilon,0}
to
u_{0}
,
and
\Gamma_{\star}
is a continuous function as discussed in
Remark
2.1
. This shows that

\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\varphi(x,y,t,t)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}(x,y,z,t)\rightarrow\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\varphi(x,y,\Gamma_{\star}(x,y,\lambda,0),\Gamma_{\star}(x,y,\lambda,0))\mathrm{d}\mu(x,y,z,t)

(2.13)

and then by comparing (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) we can
conclude that there exists a subsequence such that

\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\varphi(x,y,z,t)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}(x,y,z,t)\rightarrow\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\varphi(x,y,\Gamma_{\star}(x,y,\lambda,0),\Gamma_{\star}(x,y,\lambda,0))\mathrm{d}\mu(x,y,z,t).

We can identify the limit distribution
\mu
by noting that since

\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon})\rightarrow 1
we have

\mathbbm{E}[\psi(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon})|\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}]=\frac{\mathbbm{E}[\psi(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon})\mathbbm{I}_{\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}}]}{\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon})}\rightarrow\mathbbm{E}[\psi(u_{0},\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda))]

for any test function
\psi
. So the first two marginals of
\mu
have the
law of
(u_{0},\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda))
and they are independent since

(u_{\varepsilon,0},\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon})
are independent for any

\varepsilon
. Calling
\nu
the law of
(u_{0},\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda))
we
have that

\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\varphi(x,y,z,t)\mathrm{d}\mu(x,y,z,t)=\int_{\mathscr{C}\hskip 0.81949pt^{-\alpha}\times\mathcal{X}_{T}}\varphi(x,y,\Gamma_{\star}(x,y,\lambda,0),\Gamma_{\star}(x,y,\lambda,0))\mathrm{d}\nu(x,y)

which implies that
\mu
is unique and that the whole family

(\mu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
converges to
\mu
. We can conclude that

u^{\star}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow u^{\star}
in law with
u^{\star}=u(\lambda)
up to the time
T_{\star}\left(\|\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{X}_{T}},\|u_{0}\|_{\mathscr{C}\hskip 0.81949pt^{-1/2-\kappa}},|\lambda|\right)

since the function
T^{\star}
is lower semicontinuous (as obtained from the
a-priori estimates).

3
Convergence of the enhanced noise

This is the central section of the paper, in which we present a new method to
estimate certain random fields that do not have a finite chaos decomposition,
and we apply it to the treatment of the random fields

\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}
of (
2.2
).

3.1
An example of convergence

We choose to give first a complete example (the convergence of the tree

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}
to
Y^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(\lambda)
) in order to put in evidence
the main idea in the proof of Theorem
3.1
. Recall its
definition (
2.2
):

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}=\frac{\varepsilon^{-1}}{3}\tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon}),

with
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
being the first derivative of the
centered function
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
1.6
).
Since
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}H_{n}(x,\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon})=nH_{n-1}(x,\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon})
the Wiener chaos decomposition of

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}
reads:

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{-1}}{3}\tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{-1}}{3}\sum_{n\geqslant 3}nf_{n,\varepsilon}H_{n-1}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon},\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})

(3.1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-1}f_{3,\varepsilon}H_{2}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon},\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})+\frac{\varepsilon^{-1}}{3}\sum_{n\geqslant 4}nf_{n,\varepsilon}H_{n-1}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon},\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{3,\varepsilon}\llbracket Y_{\varepsilon}^{2}\rrbracket+\frac{\varepsilon^{-1}}{3}\sum_{n\geqslant 4}nf_{n,\varepsilon}H_{n-1}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon},\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}),

where
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
is the Wiener product and we used the
fact that
\varepsilon^{-\frac{n}{2}}H_{n}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon},\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})=\llbracket Y_{\varepsilon}^{n}\rrbracket
. Now one can use hypercontractivity (as done
in
[
10
]
,
[
11
]
) to control the

L^{p}
norm of each chaos order by its
L^{2}
norms. However this strategy does
not give useful bounds for the infinite series in the second term
of (
3.1
). Instead, we just observe that

\sum_{n\geqslant 4}nf_{n,\varepsilon}H_{n-1}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon},\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})=(\operatorname{id}-J_{0}-\ldots-J_{2})\tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon}),

where
J_{i}
is the projection of on the
i
-th chaos, and look for a different
way to write this remainder. One of the main insights of this paper is that we
can write it as:

(\operatorname{id}-J_{0}-\ldots-J_{2})\tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})=\delta^{3}G_{[1]}^{[3]}\mathrm{D}^{3}\tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})

where
\mathrm{D}
,
\delta
are the Malliavin derivative and divergence
operators, and
G_{[1]}^{[3]}=(1-L)^{-1}(2-L)^{-1}(3-L)^{-1}

with
L
the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operator. This is proven in
Lemma
B.1
.

To compute the Malliavin derivative of
\tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})
we observe that for every

\varepsilon>0

(t,x)\in\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3}
there exists

h_{(t,x)}\in L^{2}(\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3})
such that the Gaussian
random variable
Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)}:=Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x)
can
be written as

Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)}\overset{\text{law}}{=}\langle\xi,h_{(t,x)}\rangle.

(3.2)

Here
\xi
is the Gaussian white noise on
\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3}
,
which can be seen as a Gaussian Hilbert space
\langle\xi,h\rangle_{h\in H}=\{W(h)\}_{h\in H}
indexed by the Hilbert space
H:=L^{2}(\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3})
. This is the framework in which we apply
the Malliavin calculus results of Appendix
B
. Notice that by
construction

\langle h_{(t,x)},h_{(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})}\rangle=C_{\varepsilon}(t-t^{\prime},x-x^{\prime}):=\mathbbm{E}[Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)}Y_{\varepsilon,(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})}].

(3.3)

The function
h_{(t,x)}
can actually be written as the space-time
convolution

h_{(t,x)}=\check{P}\ast\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,x),

with
\check{P}(t,x)=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{|x|^{2}}{4t}}e^{-t}\mathbbm{1}_{t\geqslant 0}
and
\psi_{\varepsilon}
such that

\eta_{\varepsilon}=\psi_{\varepsilon}\ast\xi
,
\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\varepsilon^{-5/2}\psi(\varepsilon^{-2}t,\varepsilon^{-1}x)
. We
omit the dependence on
\varepsilon
of
h_{(t,x)}
not to burden the
notation.

Going back to the calculations, we obtain from (
3.2
) that

\mathrm{D}\tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{F}^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})h.
Then (noting that

\tilde{F}^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}=F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}
):

\begin{array}[]{lll}Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}&=&f_{3,\varepsilon}\llbracket Y_{\varepsilon}^{2}\rrbracket+\frac{\varepsilon^{1/2}}{3}\delta^{3}G_{[1]}^{[3]}F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon})h^{\otimes 3}\\
&=&f_{3,\varepsilon}\llbracket Y_{\varepsilon}^{2}\rrbracket+\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}},\end{array}.

(3.4)

It can be easily seen from (
3.2
) that
Y_{\varepsilon}
has
the same law of a time-space mollification of
X
by convolution (with
X

defined in Section
2.2
). Then the convergence in law of
f_{3,\varepsilon}\llbracket Y_{\varepsilon}^{2}\rrbracket
to
Y^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(\lambda)

can be easily established by standard techniques (see

[
4
]
or
[
18
]
).
We are only left to show that
\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}

in (
3.4
) converges to zero in
C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-1-\kappa}
.

It is well known (see Section
3.2
for details) that in order to
control the norm of
\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(t,\cdot)
for
t\in(0,T]
in the Besov space
\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-\alpha-\kappa}

\forall\kappa>0
and in
probability, it is enough to have suitable estimates for

\sup_{x\in\mathbbm{T}^{3}}\left\|\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(t,\bar{x})\right\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\left\|\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(t,\bar{x})\right\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},

for any
\bar{x}\in\mathbbm{T}^{3}
since
\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}
is
stationary in space. We then proceed to compute:

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(t,\bar{x})\right\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{1/2}}{3}\left\|\delta^{3}G_{[1]}^{[3]}\int K_{q,\bar{x}}(x)F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)})h_{(t,x)}^{\otimes 3}\mathrm{d}x\right\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

where
K_{q,\bar{x}}(x)
is the kernel associated to the Littlewood-Paley
block
\Delta_{q}
. Observe that
\|F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)})\|^{p}_{L^{p}}=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}|F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(z)|^{p}\gamma(\mathrm{d}z)
where
\gamma(\mathrm{d}z)
is
the density of a centered Gaussian with variance
\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon}
.
This norm is then finite by the bound (
1.8
) of
Assumption
1
.

Another fundamental idea of this work is that we can “estimate out” the
bounded term
\|F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)})\|_{L^{p}}
, which has an infinite chaos decomposition, and obtain a
standard
\Phi^{4}_{3}
diagram that can be treated with well understood
techniques. Using Lemma
B.2
and Corollary
B.6
,
one has

\displaystyle\left\|\delta^{3}G_{[1]}^{[3]}\int K_{q,\bar{x}}(x)F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)})h_{(t,x)}^{\otimes 3}\mathrm{d}x\right\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{3}\left\|\mathrm{D}^{k}G_{[1]}^{[3]}\int K_{q,\bar{x}}(x)F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)})h_{(t,x)}^{\otimes 3}\mathrm{d}x\right\|_{L^{p}(\Omega,H^{\otimes 3+k})}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|\int K_{q,\bar{x}}(x)F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)})h_{(t,x)}^{\otimes 3}\mathrm{d}x\right\|_{L^{p}(\Omega,H^{\otimes 3})}.

Then note that we can decompose the norm
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega,H^{\otimes 3})}=\|\|u\|^{2}_{H^{\otimes 3}}\|^{1/2}_{L^{p/2}}
for
u\in L^{p}(\Omega,H^{\otimes 3})
and since the norm of the Hilbert space
H^{\otimes 3}
is given by the scalar product
\|h^{\otimes 3}\|^{2}_{H^{\otimes 3}}=\langle h,h\rangle_{H}^{3}
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(t,\bar{x})\right\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{1/2}\left\|\left\|\int K_{q,\bar{x}}(x)F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)})h_{(t,x)}^{\otimes 3}\mathrm{d}x\right\|_{H^{\otimes 3}}^{2}\right\|_{L^{p/2}}^{1/2}

\lesssim\left[\varepsilon\int|K_{q,\bar{x}}(x)K_{q,\bar{x}}(x^{\prime})|\left\|F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)})F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x^{\prime})})\right\|_{L^{p/2}}|\langle h_{(t,x)},h_{(t,x^{\prime})}\rangle|^{3}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The norm containing
\tilde{F}^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}
can then be easily
estimated using Hölder’s inequality as

\left\|F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)})F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x^{\prime})})\right\|_{L^{p/2}}\leqslant\left\|F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x)})\right\|_{L^{p}}\left\|F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\varepsilon,(t,x^{\prime})})\right\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim 1.

This yields

\left\|\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(t,\bar{x})\right\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left[\varepsilon\int|K_{q,\bar{x}}(x)K_{q,\bar{x}}(x^{\prime})||\langle h_{(t,x)},h_{(t,x^{\prime})}\rangle|^{3}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

which is a standard
\Phi^{4}_{3}
diagram that can be analysed with the
techniques of
[
8
]
(recalled in
Appendix
A.3
). We just remark that
\langle h_{(t,x)},h_{(t,x^{\prime})}\rangle=C_{\varepsilon}(0,x-x^{\prime})
and the bound
\varepsilon|C_{\varepsilon}(t,x)|\lesssim 1
of Lemma
A.12
yields

\forall\delta\in(0,1)
:

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(t,\bar{x})\right\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\frac{\delta}{2}}\left[\int|K_{q,\bar{x}}(x)K_{q,\bar{x}}(x^{\prime})||C_{\varepsilon}(0,x-x^{\prime})|^{2+\delta}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\frac{\delta}{2}}2^{(1+\delta/2)q}

and then
\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(t,\cdot)
converges to zero in
probability in the space
\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-1-\delta/2}

\forall\delta\in(0,1)
,
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. The time regularity of

\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}
needed to obtain the convergence in
C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-1-\delta/2}
does not need new ideas, and it is done in
Section
3.3
.

The method shown in this section is valid verbatim for the trees

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing}
,
Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{261234}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/1_tree_mod.eps}}}
,

Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/31_tree.eps}}}
, while for the composite trees
in (
2.2
) (namely
Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/32_tree_res.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}},\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/23_tree_res.eps}}},Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{783703}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/33_tree_res.eps}}}
) that are obtained via paraproducts of simple
trees, one has to be able to write the remainder

\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}
as an iterated Skorohod integral
\delta^{n}(\ldots)
in order to exploit the boundedness of this operator. Moreover,
these trees require a second renormalization (on top of the Wick ordering)
which is not easy to control for infinite chaos decompositions. We deal with
both these difficulties introducing the product
formula (
B.6
), which allows to write products of
iterated Skorohod integrals as combinations of iterated Skorohod integrals.
The details and calculations for composite trees can be found in
Section
3.4
.

3.2
Main theorem and overview of the proof

Theorem 3.1
.

Under Assumption
1
there exists
C>0
such
that for any
p\in[2,\infty)
we have
\|\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{T}}\char 60\relax C
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
. Moreover,

\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{X}_{T}
and
Y_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow X\in C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-1/2-\kappa}
in law.

The rest of Section
3
is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem
3.1
.

From the definition
\mathbbm{Y}(\lambda)=(Y^{\tau}(\lambda))_{\tau}

of (
2.9
) it is clear that we need to prove that

Y^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow Y^{\tau}(\lambda)
for every
tree
\tau
. Note that we can write each tree
Y^{\tau}(\lambda)
as

Y^{\tau}(\lambda)=f_{\tau}(\lambda)K^{\tau}(X)
for a the measurable
function
K^{\tau}
of the Gaussian process
X\in C_{T}\mathscr{C}\hskip 1.00006pt^{-1/2-\kappa}

defined (
2.10
), and a suitable deterministic function
f_{\tau}(\lambda)
of
\lambda
. For example, we can write
Y^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(\lambda)=\lambda_{3}\llbracket X^{2}\rrbracket
with
K^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(X)=X^{\resizebox{522469}{}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}=\llbracket X^{2}\rrbracket
and

f_{\resizebox{393216}{294912}{\includegraphics[]{trees/2_tree.eps}}}(\lambda)=\lambda_{3}
.

We will show (eqs. (
3.13
)
and (
3.21
)) that every random field
Y^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}

defined in (
2.2
) can be decomposed with the same functions

f_{\tau}(\cdot)
and
K^{\tau}
as

Y^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}=f_{\tau}(\lambda_{\varepsilon})K^{\tau}(Y_{\varepsilon})+\hat{Y}^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}

(3.5)

where
\hat{Y}^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}
are suitable remainder terms. For all
p\geqslant 2
it is well-known (see
[
4
]
,

[
8
]
) that the term
f_{\tau}(\lambda_{\varepsilon})K^{\tau}(Y_{\varepsilon})
is uniformly bounded in
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathcal{X}^{\tau})
(with
\mathcal{X}^{\tau}
given by (
2.8
)).
Thus, we will prove that
\hat{Y}^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}
converges to zero in

L^{p}(\Omega;\mathcal{X}^{\tau})
. This can be done by showing that, by
Besov embedding, for
p\in[2,\infty)
and
\forall\alpha\char 60\relax|\tau|
we
have

\mathbbm{E}(\|\hat{Y}^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{\mathscr{C}\hskip 0.81949pt^{\alpha-3/p}}^{p})\lesssim\mathbbm{E}(\|\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}(t)\|^{p}_{B^{\alpha}_{p,p}})\leqslant\sum_{q}2^{\alpha pq}\int_{\mathbbm{T}^{3}}\|\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}(t,x)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\mathrm{d}x\rightarrow 0

(3.6)

thanks to the stationarity of the process
Y(t,x)
. In order to prove the
bound (
3.6
) it suffices to show that
\forall t\in[0,T]

\sum_{q}2^{\alpha pq}\sup_{x}\|\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}(t,x)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as $\varepsilon\rightarrow 0$,}

(3.7)

which is one of the key estimation of this paper and will be performed in
Sections
3.3
and
3.4.2
.

In order to obtain uniform convergence for
t\in[0,T]
it suffices to show
that
\forall\sigma\in[0,1/2]
,
q\geqslant-1
:

\sup_{x}\|\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}(t,x)-\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}(s,x)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leqslant C_{\varepsilon}|t-s|^{\sigma p}2^{-(\alpha-2\sigma)pq}\quad\text{with $C_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 0$}.

(3.8)

Indeed, by the Garsia-Rodemich-Rumsey inequality we obtain for
\delta>0

small enough and
p
large enough

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon}\mathbbm{E}(\|\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}\|^{p}_{C_{T}^{\sigma-2/p}B^{\alpha-2\sigma-\delta}_{p,p}})

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle T^{2}\sum_{q}2^{(\alpha-2\sigma-\delta)pq}\sup_{s\char 60\relax t\in[0,T]}\sup_{x}\frac{\|\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}(t,x)-\Delta_{q}\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}(s,x)\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{p}}{|t-s|^{\sigma p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon}T^{2}\sum_{q}2^{-\delta pq}

which by Besov embedding yields an estimation on
\mathbbm{E}\left(\|Y_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}\|_{C_{T}^{\sigma-\kappa/2}\mathscr{C}\hskip 0.81949pt^{\alpha-2\sigma-\kappa}}\right)
for
\kappa>0
small enough. This gives us the necessary
tightness to claim that
\mathbbm{Y}_{\varepsilon}
has weak limits along
subsequences.

The only thing left after proving (
3.5
),
(
3.7
) and (
3.8
) is that for each
\tau
we
have
K^{\tau}(Y_{\varepsilon})\rightarrow K^{\tau}(X)
in law. However
this is clear and already well-known, since by hypothesis we can introduce a
space-time convolution regularisation of
X
(let’s call it
X_{\varepsilon}
)
which has the same law of
Y_{\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
. This
yields immediately the convergence
Y_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow X
in law. At
this point an approximation argument gives that
K^{\tau}(Y_{\varepsilon})

has the same law of
K^{\tau}(X_{\varepsilon})
. Transposing the
regularisation to the kernels of the chaos expansion we can write
K^{\tau}(X_{\varepsilon})=K_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}(X)
and now it is easy to check
that
K_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}(X)\rightarrow K^{\tau}(X)
in probability
(as done systematically in
[
4
]
,

[
18
]
). We can then conclude that
K^{\tau}(Y_{\varepsilon})\rightarrow K^{\tau}(X)
and therefore

Y^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow Y^{\tau}(\lambda)
in law for every

\tau
, since from Assumption
1
we have immediately
f_{\tau}(\lambda_{\varepsilon})\rightarrow f_{\tau}(\lambda)
.

Let us give some more details on how to prove the
decomposition (
3.5
) and the bounds (
3.7
)
and (
3.8
). As seen in Section
3.1
we have

Y_{\varepsilon,\zeta}=\langle\xi,h_{\zeta}\rangle
in law for
\zeta=(t,x)\in\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3}
and this gives

\mathrm{D}^{n}\tilde{F}^{(m)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon,\zeta})=\tilde{F}^{(m+n)}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon,\zeta})h^{\otimes n}_{\zeta}.

We define for
m\in\mathbbm{N}
,
\zeta\in\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3}
:

\Phi_{\zeta}^{[m]}:=\varepsilon^{\frac{m-3}{2}}\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(m)}(\varepsilon^{1/2}Y_{\varepsilon,\zeta})

(3.9)

Note that the term
\Phi^{[m]}
above is
not
the
m
-th derivative
of some function
\Phi
(we use the square parenthesis notation to emphasize
this fact). It easy to see from (
3.9
) that
\mathrm{D}^{k}\Phi^{[m]}_{\zeta}=\Phi^{[m+k]}_{\zeta}h_{\zeta}^{\otimes k}
. Therefore,
the partial chaos expansion (
B.3
) takes a more explicit form
when applied to
\Phi_{\zeta}^{[m]}
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\Phi_{\zeta}^{[m]}&=&\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{\mathbbm{E}(\Phi^{[m+k]}_{\zeta})}{k!}\llbracket Y^{k}_{\varepsilon,\zeta}\rrbracket+\delta^{n}(G_{[1]}^{[n]}\Phi^{[m+n]}_{\zeta}h_{\zeta}^{\otimes n})\\
&=&\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\varepsilon^{(m+k-3)/2}\frac{(m+k)!}{k!}\tilde{f}_{m+k,\varepsilon}\llbracket Y^{k}_{\varepsilon,\zeta}\rrbracket+\delta^{n}(G_{[1]}^{[n]}\Phi^{[m+n]}_{\zeta}h_{\zeta}^{\otimes n})\end{array}

(3.10)

with
G_{[1]}^{[n]}
defined in (
B.1
). Here we used the fact that

\delta^{n}(h_{\zeta}^{\otimes n})=\llbracket Y^{n}_{\varepsilon,\zeta}\rrbracket
(see Remark
B.12
) and that by the definition of

\Phi^{[m]}_{\zeta}
we obtain
\forall\zeta\in\mathbbm{R}\times\mathbbm{T}^{3}
:

\mathbbm{E}(\Phi^{[m+k]}_{\zeta})=\varepsilon^{(m+k-3)/2}(m+k)!\tilde{f}_{m+k,\varepsilon}

with
\tilde{f}_{n,\varepsilon}
the coefficients in the decomposition
